Vegetation. — The  principal  forest  in  the  District  is  on  the  slopes  of 
the  DeTaraydurga  hills,  extending  over  a  tract  of  18  square  miles,  reser- 
ved as  a  State  forest.  Farther  north  the  hills  around  Kortagere  are 
clothed  with  good  fuel  jungle.  Near  Madgui  tho  vegetation  improves 
in  appearance  and  variety,  and  with  care  a  valuable  addition  might  here 
be  made  to  the  forests  of  the  District.  On  the  western  range  of  hills 
running  north  from  Kibbatthalli,  theie  is  a  forest  of  karachi  (hardwickia 
Unata),  extending  for  about  8  miles  from  Bukkapatna  northwards  to 
Gangarapente. 

The  best  wooded  taluks  are  those  which  include  tht>  great  eastern 
range  of  hills,  namely,  Tumkur,  Kortagere,  Madgiri,  and  the  old 
Huliyurdurga  taluk*  In  these  too  sandal  grows.  The  south-western 
taluks  are  well  occupied  with  trees  in  topes,  and  such  as  have  planted 
themselves  in  valleys  and  hedges.  Cocoanut  gardens  are  numerous,  and 
in  some  parts  the  butca  frondosa  grows  abundantly  In  waste  lands.  The 
north  and  centre  of  the  Sira  taluk  is  badly  supplied  with  wood,  but  the 
wild  custard  apple  grows  in  profusion  in  the  plains.

cabbages,  bean6,  peas,  ginger  :  the  latter  includes  mangoes,  oranges,  figs,

«    ates,  plantains,  limes,  citron,  guavas,  &c

Wild  Animals. — The  laiger  game  io  very  scarce,  and  pretty  nearly

Sua  taluk,  deer  may  some!  unes  be  found  and  occasional  cheetahs. 

Birds  of  prey  aie  btitod  to  be  voiy  numerous. 

Domoslio  Aniraalfi. —  \niong  bullocks  those  of  Madgiri,  Turuve- 
kere,  and  parts  ot  Kndaba  are  highly  esteemed  on  account  of  their  breed, 
but  the  cattle  generally  resemble  those  of  tho  other  Districts  of  the 
Nundydroog  Division.  JhuTalocs  aie  commonly  used  both  for  agricultu- 
ral and  dairy  purposes.  Swine  are  numerous  in  certain  localities.  Cows 
aro  plentiful,  but  poor  in  sine,  breed,  and  yield  of  milk.  Sheep  are  not 
of  the  highest  quality,  but  a  good  breed  is  met  with  in  Chiknayakanhalli 
and  the  taluks  to  the  south  and  west.  Those  of  Madgiri,  Midageai  and 
that  Bide  are  considered  less  valuable.  The  live  "stock  of  the  District  is 
thus  returned  :— cows  and  bullocks  291,989,  horses  784,  ponies  4,270, 
donkeys  5,051,  sheep  and  goats  353,385,  pigs  7,159.

The  overthrow  of  the  Vijayanagar  empire  on  the  .field  of  Talikota  * 
in  1584  opened  the  way  for  many  invaders.  The  Bijapnr  army  under 
Ban-dalha  Khan  overran  all  the  north  of  the  District  in  1638,  and  Sira,,  • 
with  D.  Ballapur,  Bangaloro,  Hoskote,  and  Kolar,  forming  what  was  called 
Carnatic  Bijapur,  were  placed  under  the  government  of  ShahjL  On  the] 
capture  of  Oolconda  and  Bijapur  by  Aurangzib  in  1687,  and  the  conquest 
of  these  territories  by  the  Mogal  army,  Sira  was  made  tho  capital  of  the 
new  province,— consisting  of  the  seven  parganas  of  Basvapatna,  iiudihal, 
Sira,  Pennakonda,  Dodballapur,  Hoskolc,  and  Kolar, — placed  under 
iOiasun  Khan  as  Subadar  or  Foujdar  of  tlio  Carnalic.  This  officer  applied 
himself  with  energy  and  success  to  the  task  of  regulating  and  im]  awing 
the  district.  In  1G98  he  was  killed  at  Dodori,  and  the  distinguished 
general  Zulfikar  Khan  succcedod.  Another  governor  named  Eustam 
Jang  is  said  to  have  built  the  foit  and  petta,  and  by  hi',  wiso  administra- 
tion of  affairs  to  have  obtained  tho  title  of  Bahadur  and  the  name  of  , 
Kaifiyat  Khan.  In  1757  Sira  was  taken  by  tho  Mahrattas,  and  restored 
two  years  after  on  the  conclusion  oi  peace  with  Mjsoro.  hi  1701  it  was 
taken  hy  Ilaidar,  in  allium  o  with  Basalat  Jann,  who  had  conferred  upon 
him  the  title  of  Nav.ib  of  Bira.  In  1766  it  fell  again  into  tho  hands  of 
tho  Mahrattas  by  tbo  defection  of  Ilaidai's  biother,  and  in  1 774  was 
reconquered  by  Tippu.  Tho  Mahrattas  once  more  occupied  it  for  a 
short  time  in  1791  on  m*irching  to  join  tbo  anny  ot  Lnd  Coniwallis. 

At  tho  liegiuiiing  of  the  present  century  tho  District  was  embraced 
in  tho  Madgiri  roujdari.  After  the  British  assumption  m  1832  the 
Tumkfir  District  was  formed,  and  with  that  of  Chitaldioog  constituted 
the  Chitaldroog  Division.  At  tho  reorganization  of  1863  this  Division 
was  buoken  up,  and  Tuinkur  became  one  of  the  Districts  of  the  new 
Nundydroog  Division. 

POPULATION. 

Number. — The  population  of  the  District  is  G32,239,  consisting  of 
315,440  males  and  316,799  females. 

Density. — This  gives  175"3  persons  to  the  square  mile.  The  most 
thickly  peopled  taluks'  at  the  time  of  the  census  were  Iluliyurdurga 
(since  united  with  Kunigal),  where  the  number  was  262  to  the  square 
mile,  Tfimkur  and  Kortagero,  whore  the  rate  was  2  5S  and  2  53  respectively.

Increase, — On  reverting  to  the  hhdneshmdri  returns  for  1853—54, 
we  ascertain  that  the  population  of  each 
taluk  was  then  estimated  according  to  the 
statement  in  tho  margin.  Comparing  the 
total  of  390,420  with  that  of  632,239 
obtained  at  the  census  of  1871,  an  increase 
apparently  took  place  of  59'4  percent  in  the 
18  years.  If  these  data  are  reliable  they 
bear  witness  to  a  high  degree  of  pros- 
perity during  tho  last  quarter  of  a  century,  but  the  old  figures  were  per- 
haps too  low.

Xhafhs.— -The  nunibor  of  deaths  for  the  same  period  was  6,300,  be* 
hjg  3,311  males,  and  2,093  females.  01  the  total  number,  6  deaths  oc- 
ttarred  among  Christians,  277  among  Muhammadans,  5.685  among  Hin- 
dus, and  338  among  other  clas>se=.  According  to  age :  there  wore  born 
deacl 72,  died  under  I  jear  665,  from  1  to  5jeais  546,fiom  C  to  12  years 
406,  from  12  to  20  years  526,  ftom  21  to  30  yearb  824,  from  31  to  40 
years  752,  from  41  to  50  ycat&  728,  from  51  to  60  years  732,  over  60 
years  1,055. 

The  causes  of  death  were :  from  dismsfi, — cholera  3,  small  pox  480, 
fevers  3,577,  bowol  complaints  452 ;  from  injuria, — suicide  7,  wounds 
14,  accidents  113,  snake  bite  or  wild  beasts  19  ;  all  other  causes  1,641.

TRADE. 

The  trade  of  the  District,  though  followed  by  many  other  castes  or 
classes,  is  principally  iu  the  hands  of  the  Lingayats.  Their  chief  emporium 
is  at  Gubbi,  but  there  are  also  extensive  marts  at  Bellavi,  Turvekere, 
Tiptur  and  Chiknayakanhalli,  to  which  the  trade  of  the  southern 
Mahratta  country,  and  of  the  Bellari,  Yellore,  and  Madras  districts  is 
attracted,  as  well  as  that  of  the  Nagar  and  Ashtagram  Divisions.  The 
Lbgayat  merchants  gener.Uly  have  either  extensive  connections  or  branch/ 
agencies  at  Dharwar  and  Nagar. 

The  articles  of  merchandise  and  course  of  trade  may  be  gathered 
from  the  following  statement

Nagar  and  transmitted  to  Vellore  and  Wallaja;  whence  nrftmegs,  > 
mace  and  European  cloths  are  obtained  in  exchange  and  vjcpajfaQ,  fe> ' 
Nagar.   Sugar,  sugar  candy  and  Bilk,  the  produce  of  Bangalore,  i 
with  cocoa  nuts  raised  on  the  spot,  are  sent  to  Dharwar ;  whence* 
a^  thread  are  received  in  return,  part  of  which  goes  to  Nagat.

Chan-narayan-durga  or  Climina-rdyah-durga. — A  conspicuous 
but  deserted  hdl  fort  in  tbe  Koitagui  taluk,  situated  midway  between 
Kortagiri  and  Madgiri.  Foimeily  the  head  quarteis  of  a  taluk  bear- 
ing the  same  name.  It  appears  to  have  been  originally  fortified  by 
Chikkappa  Gauda  of  Madgui,  and  named  Allimensti-kallu.  In  his 
family  it  remained  for  a  long  penod,  when  it  was  taken  by  the  Mahrattas, 
rand  Rama  Gauda,  at  that  time  the  chief,  took  iefuge  in  Madgiri* 
In  two  years  however  he  retook  it,  but  only  to  hold  it  for  eight  years, 
longer.  It  was  then  captured  by  Koioaraiya,  general  of  the  Mysore 
Eaja,  and  Dodda  Arasu  plated  in  charge.  Its  name  was  also  changed 
to  Prasanna-giu.  It  was  subsequently  taken  by  Haidar,  and  after '6 
years  by  the  Mahrattas  under  Madhava  Iiao,  and  after  a  like  period 
retaken  by  Tippu. 

Devaraydurga. — A  foitmed  hill  U  miles  east  of  Tiimkur,  situated 
amidst  wild  and  picturesque  scenery,  and  a  hot  weather  retreat  for  the 
European  residents.  It  consists  of  thiee  distinct  terraces  or  elevations, 
and  is  well  supplied  with  springs.  Near  the  summit,  which  is  3,940 
feet  above  the  level  of  the  sea,  is  a  small  temple  connected  with  the  princi- 
pal one  on  tho  lower  level  dedicated  to  Durga  Narasimha.  On  the 
intermediate  terrace  are  three  European  houses.  The  fortifications  are 
carried  round  the  three  elevations,  and  must  in  furmei  days  have  been  of 
considerable  strength.  Tradition  relates  that  a  robber  chief  named 
Andhaka  or  Lingaka  had  his  stronghold  here,  and  that  he  was  subdued 
by  Sumati,  a  prince  whose  father  Homachandra  was  king  of  Karnata  and 
ruled  in  Yadupatna.  On  thus  accomplishing  the  enterprise  on  which  he 
hadbeon  sent,  Sumati  is  related  to  have  established  the  city  of  BhumaU' 
dana  near  the  present  Nelamangala  (Bangalore  District),  and  taken  up 
his  residence  thero  for  the  piotection  of  that  pari  of  his  father's  kingdom. 
The  hill  was  in  the  possession  of  a  chief  named  Jataka,  and  called  Jata-

TUMKUH  DIBTBIOT. 


*  •  GuMa  has  suffered  much  from  the  party  spirit  of  the  contending 
sects  of  Komatis  and  Banajigas  or  Lingayats,  and  was  at  one  time  itt 
immiaent  danger  of  abandonment  from  this  cause.  Daring  th3  administra- 
tion of  Purnaiya,  the  erection  of  a  temple  by  the  Komatis  to  a  virgin, 
who  had  cast  hersolf  into  the  flames  to  avoid  dishonour,  excited  the 
"previous  animosity  of  the  opposite  party  to  such  a  degree  that, 
the  town  was  ordered  to  be  divided  by  a  wall  in  order  to  separate 
the  quarters  of  the  two  factious.  An  Amildar  was  at  length  chosen 
,from  the  party  which  apposed  least  m  the'wiong,  and  with  the  help 
of  the  Brahma  ns  and  peaceably  disposed  inhabitants  order  was  gradually 
restored,  although  the  exticme  measure  of  slaughtering  an  ass  in  the 
main  street  was  threatened,  winch  would  have  compelled  all  Hindus 
to  abandon  the  town. 

AfcGubbi  is  one  of  llic  chief  annual  fairs  in  the  country,  frequent- 
ed  by  merchants  fioin  gioat  dibtances.  The  neighborhood  produces 
coarse  cotton  cloths,  both  white  and  colored,  blankets,  sack-cloth,  areca 
nutof  the  kind  called  wolagra  (or  neighbouring),  cocoa  nuts,  jaggory,  tama- 
rind, capsicum,  wheat,  lice,  ragi,  and  other  grains  ;  lac,  steel,  aqd.  iron  ; 
all  of  wliich  here  find  a  ready  sale  or  exchange,  as  the  imports  are  large, 
and  it  is  an  intermediate,  mart  lor  all  goods  passing  through  the 
Peninsula  in  almost  every  direction.  It  is  computed  that  at  the  weekly 
fair  there  ato  sold  from  50  to  100  loads  of  aroca  nut  produced 
in  the  neighbouring  districts  of  Sira,  Ilagalvadi,  Chiknayakaiihalli, 
Budihal,  Honnavalli  and  Gubbi  ;as  well  as  from  20  to  40  loads 
of  kopri  or  dry  cocot\  nut  from  tho  saino  districts.  Taking  the  load 
at  8  m.iunds  of  10  Sultani  seers,  there  aie  thus  sold  annually  ht  this 
mart  alono  335  tons  (f  aicc.i  nut,  and  134  tons  of  cocoa  nut,  the 
produce  of  the  noighbnuihood,  uptcocnting  a  money  value  oi  Rs.  2,1 8,400 
andRs.  33,280  lospothvely. 

Of  tho  coarse  cloths  made  in  the  vicinity  by  tho  Devacgas,  Togatas

ven it  off  and  puisued  it  with  the  other  cattle  and  their  owners  until  it 
was  killed.  The  courage  of  the  cattle  was  attubuted  to  the  peculiar  virtue 
Of  the  spot,  pointing  it  out  as  suitable  lor  the  construction  of  a  fort 
Accordingly  the  carcase  of  the  tiger  being  burnt,  tbe  limits  of  the  fort

as  at  Halobid,  Bdlui,  &c.  Tradition  relates  Hut  Jakaunch.iri's  career 
began  while  Nripa  Raya  was  rnlmg  in  Kridapiira.  Ho  then  left  his 
ri&tive  place  and,  entc  riuq  the  ■•ervico  ot  v  arious  eoui  ts,  produced  the  works 
by  which  his  lame  is  tothib  day  upheld.  Atter  his  dcpaituie  a  son 
Dankanachari  was  boin  to  hiui,  who,  when  grown  up,  set  out  iu  search 
of  his  father,  neither  having  e\er  seen  the  otlicr.  At  Belur  the  young 
man  found  tho  Chcmialcob'ava  lemplo  in  course  of  oredion  and — so  the 
story  goes,  — lcmarked  that  one  ol  Ik  images  hail  a  blemish.  As  this  would 
be  fatal  to  its  claim  as  an  object,  of  wotship,  tho  architect,  who  was  no 
other  than  Jakanachari  himself,  hastily  vowed  to  cut  ofl'his  right  hand  if 
any  defect  could  be  found  in  an  lma^e  he  had  carved.  To  test  the  matter 
the  figure  was  covered  with  Kindal  paste,  which  diied  on  eveiy  part  except 
around  the  navel.  Iu  this  ou  examination  was  found  a  cavity  the  son  had 
detected,  containing  a  frog  and  some  sand  "ind  water,  Mortitied  at  the 
result  Jakanachari  cut  oil'  his  right  hand,  and  inquiries  as  to  who  his 
critic  was,  led  to  the  unexpected  discovery  of*  their  mutual  relationship. 
Subsequently  Jakanachari  was  duecttd  in  a  vision  to  dedicate  a  temple 
to  the  god  Kesava  in  Kiidapiua,  bis  nauvo  place.  Thither  he  accordingly 
returned,  and  no  sooner  was  the  temple  completed  than  his  right  hand

been  called  Kai-dala,  the  restored  hand.

The  derivation  of  the  name  is  said  to  be  hmi-gattu,  dancing  stone, , 
Siva  hiring  danced  here.  Three  streams  rising  in  Sivaganga,  whose 
names  are  Nalini,  Nagini  andKainala,  are  said  here  to  unite  their  waters. 
Their  virtue  is  such  that  a  king  from  the  north,  named  Nriga  chakravarti, 
is  said  to  have  been  cared  of  leprosy  by  bathing  in  them,  and  to  have 
constructed  the  large  tank  in  consequence.  A  party  of  seven  Lipi  j6gis, 
it  is  Said,  subsequently  came  from  the  Himalayas  and  obtained  a  large 
treasure  from  the  bed  of  the  tank  after  killing  the  seven  headed 
serpent  which  guarded  it.  'The  foundation  of  the  fort  is  attributed  to  a 
Vira  Eambala  Raya  in  1290.  It  afterwards  came  into  the  possession 
of  the  Magadi  chief,  who  enlarged  the  town  and  fort.  Owing  to  a  low 
type  of  fever  the  place  has  latterly  been  very  unhealthy.  It  contains  an 
important  establishment  for  the  breeding  of  horses  for  the  Mysore  Siledars.

It  is  surroundod  on  all  sides  by  hills,  the  continuation  of  the  north 
and  south  range  which  traverses  the  east  of  the  District  The  town 
derives  its  name  from  Madhu-giri,  or  honey  hill,  at  the  northern  base  of 
which  it  is  situated.  The  two  large  temples  of  Ve,nkat  Ramana-swami 
and  Mallesvara,  standing  side  by  side,  are  conspicuous  objects.  A,  very 
graceful  ornament  is  carried  round  under  the  eaves  of  the  roof  of  the  latter, 
representing  doves  or  pigeons,  of  about  life  size,  in  every  natural  attitude. 


1UDG1BL  J  177 

The  erection  of  the  original  fort  and  town  is(  ascribed  to  a  local 
chief  named  Raja  Hira  Gau4a.  The  circumstance  of  a  stray  sheep  hav- 
ing returned  from  the  hill  dripping  with  vet,  led  to  the  discovery  that  it 
was  well  supplied  with  springs  of  water.  This  being  reported  by  the 
shepherds  of  the  neighbouring  village  of  Bijavara,  the  advantages  of  the 
situation  were  so  apparent  that  the  town  was  established,  and  mod  fortifi- 
cations constructed  on  the  hill  for  its  protection.  About  1678,  while 
Rama  Gauda'and  Timina  Gauda,  descendants  of  the  founder,  were  ruling 
in  Majgiri  taluk,  they  incurred  the  hostility  of  the  Baja  of  Mysore.  The 
daUvai  Devaraj  a  was  thorcfore  sent  against  it,  who,  after  a  siege  of  a 
year,  took  the  place  and  led  the  gaudas  with  their  iauulie*.  captive  to 
Seringapatam.  They  were,  howe\  ci,  released  and  sent  back  ro  Midagesi, 
which  was  left  to  them  out  of  their  lost  possessions. 

The  fortifications  of  Madgiri  were  greatly  mci  cased  by  Haid/ir  All 
and  the  place  was  the  scat  of  a  valuable  trade,  containing  a  hundred 
houses  of  weavers  alone,  llithcj  in  1703  on  the  c  omjuest  of  Bednur  he 
sent  as  prisoners  both  the  Itani  and  her  lovei,  as  well  as  the  pretender 
Chenna  Basavaiya  for  whose  iit>hts  he  had  ostensibly  been  lighting,  and 
here  they  remained  until  the  captine  of  the  place  by  tho  Mahiattas  in 
17G7.  The  Main"  itta  ihief  Madlu'v  j  llao  held  pob&osbion  ot  Marlgiri  for 
seven  years,  and  when  forced  by  Tippu  in  1771  to  letho,  plimdeied  the 
town  of  everything  ho  could  cairj  an  ay.  With  the  usual  exaggeration 
its  wealth  is  said  to  ha\o  been  so  gieat  that  ho  disdained  to  lcinove  any- 
thing less  valuable  than  gold.  Tippu  bestoncd  on  it  the  name  of 
Fattahabad,  city  of  viclory,  and  nude  it  the  tapilal  of  a  surrounding 
district  jiclding  a  revenue  of  .six  lakhs  of  \  at^odas.  But  his  exactions 
had  nearly  ruiued  (bo  place,  when  the  detraction  >\as  completed  by  the 
Mahrattas  in  1791.  Balvanl,  IUo.  ouo  of  Paia.u  Rani  Bhno's  officers, 
besieged  it,  though  without  sut  cess,  for  tivo  months,  haung  under  his 
command  a  large  army,  according  to  nuthe  accounts,  of  12O.000  men- 
It  was  principally  composed  of  banditti  assembled  by  the  palegars  for- 
merly driven  from  their  strongholds,  who  had  veuttued  back  under  the 
protection  of  Lord  Cornwallis.  On  the  conclusion  of  peace  they  were 
speedily  dispersed  by  the  Sultan,  but  not  befoie  thoy  had  devastated  all 
the  neighbouring  country.  Of  500  Mahratta  horse  who  had  joined  this 
rabble,  it  is  said  that  only  20  men  with  their  chief  escaped.  On  the  con- 
clusion of  the  third  Mysore  war  and  the  death  of  Tippu,  Madgiri  was  in- 
cluded in  the  new  territory  of  Mysore.

Sibi — A  village  in  tho  Sira  taluk,  15  miles  north  of  Turnkur,  on 
the  Tumkur-Chitaldroop  load.   Number  of  houses  129.  Population  754. 

It  is  remarkable  only  for  tho  temple  of  Narasimha,  at  which  there 
is  a  great  annual  festival  in  the  month  of  Magna,  attended  by  10,000 
people.  The  origin  of  the  temple  is  Urns  related.  In  the  days  when  there 
were  no  roads  and  the  place  was  coveted  with  jungle,  a  certain  merchant 
carrying  grain  on  pack  bullocks  halted  at  Sjbi.  But  when  his  pot  of  rice 
was  sot  on  to  boil  on  a  small  projecting  rock,  its  contents  turned  to  the 
colour  of  blood  and  he  with  lr.s  attendants  and  bullocks  fell  down  in  a 
swoon.  "Whdc  in  this  unconscious  state  Narasimha  appeared  to  him  in  a 
vision  and,  revealing  that  the  stone  was  his  abode,  commanded  the  mer- 
chant to  build  a  temple  over  it  in  atonement  for  the  desecration  committed. 

The  small  temple  then  erected  was  replaced  by  the  present  large 
building  during  this  century  under  the  following  circumstances.  Three 
brothers  living  at  Tdmkfir,  who  had  enriched  themselves  by  farming  the 
revenue  of  the  district  in  the  days  of  Tippu,  subsequently  sought  to  atone

for  their  oppressions  by  works  of  charily.  To  Naljfappa  the  eldest,  Nara- 
simha  in  a  vision  offered  eternal  happiness  on  condition  of  his  bnildiog* 
and  endowing  the  temple  at  Sibi.  This  was  accordingly  accomplished 
in  ten  years  by  the  three  brothers,  It  is  an  ordinary  structure  surround-

rupees.

The  foundation  ot  the  town  aud  fort  is  attributed  to  Rangappa  Nayak, 
the  chief  of  Rutuagiri,  the  selection  of  the  site  being  due,  as  is  commonly 
related  of  other  lbrts  in  the  country,  to  the  turning  of  u  hare  upon  the 
hounds  while  in  pursuit— un  indication  of  heroic  soil.  liefore  the  fort 
was  completed,  Sira  aud  its  dependencies  were  conquered  by  Ran-dulha 
Khan,  general  of  the  Bijapur  state.  Malik  Husen,  then  appointed 
governor,  completed  the  fort  and  enclosed  the  town  with  mud  walls. 

The  capture  of  Bijapnr  by  Aurangzib  in  1 G87  was  speedily  followed 
by  the  conquest  of  the  Camatic  districts  dependent  on  it.  Sira  was  made

last  of  the  Mogal„governors  of  Sim,  which  shared  in  many  of  the  contests 
between  the  rivals  for  the  Subadari  of  the  Dekhan,  was  'Dilavar  Khan, 
from  whom  the  place  was  taken  in  1757  by  the  Mahrattas. 
,  *  In  1761,  Haidar  having  entered  into  alliance  at  Hoskote  with  Basa- 
lat  Jang  and  received  from  liini  the  title  of  Navab  of  Sira,  at  once  took  the 
place  and  thence  extended  his  conquests  all  over  the  north.  In  1766 
the  brother-in-law  of  Haidar  was.  induced  by  the  Mahrattas  to  yield  up 
Sira,  which  remained  in  their  hands  till  retaken  by  Tippu  in  1774,  since 
which  time  it  has  been  attached  to  Mysore,  except  for  a  short  period  when 
it  was  occupied  by  the  Mahratta  army  co-operating  with  the  British 
against  Seringapatam. 

Sira  attained  its  highest  prosperity  under  Dilavar  Khan  and  is 
said  to  have  contained  50,000  houses.  An  elegant  palace  erected  by  him, 
now  in  ruins,  was  the  model  on  which  those  of  Bangalore  and  Seringa- 
patam were  built  The  ruins  of  a  large  quarter,  to  which  tradition  assigns 
the  name  of  Latapura,  may  jet  be  seen  to  the  north-west  of  the  fort 
'Tippu  forcibly  transported  12,000  families  from  Sira  to  form  a  population 
for  his  new  town  of  Shahar  Ganjam  on  the  island  of  Seringapatam.  These 
vicissitudes  and  the  inroads  of  the  Mahrattas  reduced  the  town  to  3,000 
houses.  There  are  now  only  671,  much  scattered.  The  Jamma  Masjid 
of  hewn stone  is  deserving  of  mention.  The  fort,  well  built  of  stone,  is 
surrounded  with  a  moat  and  a  fine  glacis. 

A  large  tank  to  the  north  irrigates  the  subjacent  lands.  The 
soil  around  is  favourable  to  the  growth  of  the  cocoa  nut,  the  dried  kernel 
of  which  is  the  staple  article  of  export  The  population  consists  largely  of
